[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)^{p}]^{\mathrm{vir}}_{0}=0_{\widetilde{\sigma}}^{!}[\mathfrak{C}_{0}]=0^{!}[0_{\mathcal{E}_{2}}],

where
0_{\mathcal{E}_{2}}
is the zero section of
\mathcal{E}_{2}|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)}
. By Lemma 4.3 in
[
CL15
]
with the complex
R:=R^{\bullet}\widehat{\pi}^{p,\circ}_{*}\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k}
and
Theorem

7.5.3
, part 3 we get

[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)^{p}]^{\mathrm{vir}}_{0}=c_{\rm top}(R^{1}\widehat{\pi}^{p,\circ}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\otimes-k}\otimes\omega_{\widehat{\pi}^{p,\circ}}))\cdot[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)].

By Serre duality we have that

c_{1-g+kd}(R^{1}\widehat{\pi}^{p,\circ}_{*}(\mathcal{L}^{\otimes-k}\otimes\omega_{\widehat{\pi}^{p}}))=(-1)^{1-g+kd}c_{1-g+kd}(R^{0}\widehat{\pi}^{p}_{*}\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k})

and thus

[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)^{p}]^{\mathrm{vir}}_{0}=(-1)^{1-g+kd}c_{1-g+kd}(R^{0}\widehat{\pi}^{p}_{*}\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k})\cdot[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)].

This proves the claim.
∎

Conjecture 7.5.7
.

Let
X
be a threefold which is a complete intersection in projective space. Then

\deg[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)^{p}]_{i}^{\mathrm{vir}}=c_{i}\deg[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\circ}_{g_{i},n}(X,d)]^{\mathrm{vir}},

for some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
g_{i}<g
.

Remark 7.5.8
.

The conjecture has been proved for genus one
[
Zin09a
,
Zin09b
,
Zin08
]
,
[
CL15
]
,
[
LO21
,
LO22
]
and genus two
[
LLO22
]
.

In genus
g=1
and
X
a Calabi–Yau threefold, the conjecture translates into

\deg[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}(X,d)]=\deg[\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\circ}_{1,n}(X,d)]^{\mathrm{vir}}+\frac{1}{12}\deg[\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,n}(X,d)]^{\mathrm{vir}}.

8.
Generalizations

The constructions above also work for quasi-maps. We first summarise some of the results in
[
CFK10
,
CFKM14
]
and then extend the definition of reduced invariants to quasi-maps and to more general targets.

8.1.
Stable quasi-maps to GIT quotients of vector spaces

Let
C
be a scheme.
Let
V
be a vector space and
G
a reductive algebraic group acting on
V
. For
P
a principal
G
-bundle on
C
we define the associated
V
-bundle a

P\times_{G}V:=(P\times V)/G.

This is a bundle with fiber
V
over
C
.
The quotient is taken by considering the action
g\cdot(p,v)=(p\cdot g^{-1},g\cdot v)
.

A morphism from
C
to the stack quotient
[V/G]
corresponds to a
G
-equivariant morphism
P\to V
from some
G
-torsor
P
over
C
to
V
. That is maps
C\to[V/G]
correspond to sections of the vector bundle
P\times_{G}V\to C
.

Let
\chi(G)
be the character group of
G
and
\theta\in\chi(G)
a fixed character. The character
\theta:G\to\mathbb{C}^{*}
determines a one-dimensional representation
\mathbb{C}_{\theta}
of
G
, hence a linearization of the trivial line bundle on
V
, which we denote by
L_{\theta}
. This is used to construct a GIT quotient

X=V/\!\!/_{\theta}G

which we assume to be proper. The GIT quotient comes with a polarization

V^{s}\times_{G}(L_{\theta}\mid_{V^{s}})=:\mathcal{O}(\theta)\to V/\!\!/_{\theta}G.

Multiples of the chosen character define the same underlying GIT quotients with a multiple of the polarization.

Let

V^{s}=V^{s}(\theta)\quad\text{and}\quad V^{ss}=V^{ss}(\theta)

be the open subsets of stable (respectively, semistable) points determined by
L_{\theta}
.
We also assume:

(1)

\emptyset\neq V^{s}=V^{ss}
and

(2)

G acts freely on
V^{s}
.

Recall that for
(C,P,u)
a curve with a
G
-torsor
P
and a section
u
of the associated bundle
P\times_{G}V
, we say that
(P,u)
has “class”
\beta\in\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Pic}([V/G]),\mathbb{Z})
if
\beta

is the composite map

\mathrm{Pic}([V/G])\xrightarrow{u^{*}}\mathrm{Pic}(C)\xrightarrow{\deg}\mathbb{Z}.

Definition 8.1.1
.

Let
X
be as before,
n,g
be positive integers and
\beta\in\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Pic}([V/G]),\mathbb{Z})
. An
n
-pointed, genus
g
quasimap of class
\beta
to
X

consists of the data

(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},P,u),

where

(1)

(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
is a connected, at most nodal,
n
-pointed projective curve of genus
g
,

(2)

P
is a principal
G
-bundle on
C
,

(3)

u
is a section of the induced vector bundle
P\times_{G}V
on
C
, such that
(P,u)
is of class
\beta
,

satisfying the following generic nondegeneracy condition:

•

there is a finite (possibly empty) set
B\subset C
such that
for every
p\in C\backslash B
we have
u(p)\in V^{s}
.

The quasimap
(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},P,u)
is called prestable
if the set of base points
B
is disjoint from the nodes and markings on C.

In
[
CFKM14
, Def. 7.1.1]
, the authors define the length of the prestable quasimap
{q}
at the point
x\in C
, as follows:

Definition 8.1.2
.

The length of a prestable quasimap
(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},P,u)
to
V/\!\!/_{\theta}G
at
x\in C
is

(69)

\ell(x)\coloneqq\min\left\{\frac{\mathrm{ord}\,_{x}(u^{*}s)}{m}\colon s\in H^{0}(V,L_{m\theta})^{G},u^{*}s\neq 0,m>0\right\}.

Definition 8.1.3
.

Given a positive rational number
\epsilon
, a quasimap
(C,p_{1},\ldots,p_{k},P,u)
is called
\epsilon
-stable if it is prestable and

(1)

the line bundle
\omega_{C}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i})\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\epsilon}
, where

\mathcal{L}:=u^{*}(P\times_{G}L_{\theta})

is ample

(2)

\epsilon\ell(x)\leq 1
, for any
x\in C
.

Theorem 8.1.4
.

[
CFKM14
, Theorem 5.2.1, Theorem 7.1.6]
The moduli space of
\epsilon
-stable quasimaps
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
is a proper DM stack with a perfect obstruction theory.

Let
\mathfrak{Bun}
denote the moduli stack of
G
-bundles over prestable curves, let
\pi:\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{Bun}
be the universal curve and
\mathfrak{P}
the universal principal bundle on
\mathfrak{C}
. Let
\mathfrak{L}:=\mathfrak{P}\times_{G}L_{\theta}
and let
\mathfrak{Bun}^{\rm st,\epsilon}
be open locus in
\mathfrak{Bun}
where

\omega_{\pi}(\sum p_{i})\otimes\mathfrak{L}^{\epsilon}

is ample.
By
[
CFKM14
, Theorem 3.2.5, and Section 4.2]
, we have an open substack
\mathfrak{Bun}^{\rm st,\epsilon}_{\beta}\subset\mathfrak{Bun}
, such that the forgetful morphism
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)\to\mathfrak{Bun}
factors through
\mathfrak{Bun}^{\rm st,\epsilon}_{\beta}
and such that
\mathfrak{Bun}^{\rm st,\epsilon}_{\beta}
which is of finite type.

As in
7.1.1
, we fix
g,n,\beta,\epsilon
and we omit the index from the notation.

Let
\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{P}}=\mathfrak{P}\times_{G}V
be the associated vector bundle on
\mathfrak{C}
. Let

\mathfrak{F}:=R^{1}\pi_{*}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathfrak{P}}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\pi})

on
\mathfrak{Bun}
. By definition we have that

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)\subset\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathrm{Sym}\,\mathfrak{F}

is an open subset obtained by imposing the open conditions in
Definition

8.1.3
.

Remark 8.1.5
.

Particular examples of this construction are:

•

toric varieties, with
\mathfrak{Bun}\simeq\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d_{1}}\times_{\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}}\cdots\times_{\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}}\mathfrak{Pic}_{g,n,d_{k}}
and
k
the rank of
{\rm Pic}(X)
;

•

Grasmannians
\mathrm{Gr}(k,r)
, with
\mathfrak{Bun}
the moduli space of rank
k
-bundles over prestable curves;

•

complete intersections in projective spaces;

8.2.
Reduced invariants for quasimaps to GIT quotients

In the following we define reduced invariants for the targets in the previous section.

As in
Section

7.4
, we define

(70)

\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}:=\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathfrak{F}}\mathfrak{Bun}\xrightarrow{p}\mathfrak{Bun}.

By construction
(p^{*}\mathfrak{F})^{\mathrm{tf}}
is locally free. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
by the Cartesian diagram

(71)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Bun}\text{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ulcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

One can see that we have an open embedding

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathrm{Sym}\,_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}}\left(p^{*}\mathfrak{F}\right)=p^{*}\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathrm{Sym}\,_{\mathfrak{Bun}}(\mathfrak{F}).

Let
\overline{\pi}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
be the universal curve over
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
,
\mathcal{P}
be the universal
G
-torsor over
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{P}\times_{G}V
. Similarly, define
{\mathcal{V}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}
, using
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
on
\widehat{\pi}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
. By
[
CL12
]
the morphism
\mu
has a perfect obstruction theory equal to
R^{\bullet}\overline{\pi}_{*}\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{P}}
. By
Equation

71
the morphism
\widetilde{\mu}
has a dual perfect obstruction theory equal to

R^{\bullet}\widehat{\pi}_{*}{\mathcal{V}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}.

As before, these perfect obstruction theories induce virtual classes

[\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)]^{\mathrm{vir}}:=\mu^{!}[\mathfrak{Bun}]\in A_{*}(\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta))

and

[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)]^{\mathrm{vir}}:=\widetilde{\mu}^{!}[\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}]\in A_{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)).

The following is a generalisation of Assumption
7.2.1
.

Assumption 8.2.1
.

In the following we assume
\beta\in\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{Pic}([V/G]),\mathbb{Z})
is such that

H^{1}(C,P\times_{G}V)=0

for any
C
smooth.

From now on we work under the Assumption
8.2.1
. Let
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
be the closure the locus in
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
which consists of points
(C,p_{1},\ldots p_{n},P,u)
, with
C
a smooth curve. We call this the main component of
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\epsilon}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
. We define the main component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
as the closure of the locus which consists of points
(C,p_{1},\ldots p_{n},P,u)
, with
C
a smooth curve. Note that this makes sense even when
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Pic}}
does not have a modular interpretation, since
p
is birational. Note that we have a commutative diagram

(72)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \overline{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Bun}\text{.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mu}^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

By
8.2.1
,
\widetilde{\mu}^{\circ}
is a smooth morphism, in fact it is just the projection of a geometric vector bundle. This makes
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
a smooth stack over
\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}
, and thus an equidimensional DM stack, so we define

[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)]^{\mathrm{vir}}:=[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)]\in A_{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)).

Definition 8.2.2
.

Let
\gamma_{i}\in A^{*}(X)
, we call
reduced
quasimap invariants of
X
, the following numbers

\int_{[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)]^{\mathrm{vir}}}\prod ev^{*}\gamma_{i}.

We deduce the following proposition analogous to
Proposition

7.4.3
which states that these invariants do not depend of the choice of the blow-up.

Proposition 8.2.3
.

Let
p^{\prime}:\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{Bun}
and
p^{\prime\prime}:\widetilde{\mathfrak{Bun}}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathfrak{Bun}
be birational projective maps such that
(p^{\prime*}\pi_{*}\mathfrak{F})^{\mathrm{tf}}
(resp.
(p^{\prime\prime*}\pi_{*}\mathfrak{F})^{\mathrm{tf}}
) and for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Consider
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)^{\prime\prime}
defined analogously to
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
above. Then we have

\int_{[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)^{\prime}]^{\mathrm{vir}}}\prod ev^{*}\gamma_{i}=\int_{[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)^{\prime\prime}]^{\mathrm{vir}}}\prod ev^{*}\gamma_{i}.

Remark 8.2.4
.

Note that as in
Remark

7.3.4
and
Remark

7.4.2
there are different ways of defining the main component of the moduli space of maps (or quasimaps) and we use both definitions.

Definition

8.2.2
and
Definition

7.4.4
are related, but possibly different in the following sense:

(1)

The reduced
invariants
agree when both definitions make sense. See
Remark

7.4.2
.

(2)

Assumption
8.2.1
is usually stronger than
7.2.1
. For example for a rational curve with normal bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-d)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-d)
Assumption
8.2.1
is not satisfied, while
7.2.1
is satisfied for large
d
.

(3)

The main components
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
in
7.4.1
and in
Section

8.2
are the same when
X
is a projective space.

(4)

The main components
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\circ}_{g,n}(X,\beta)
in
7.4.1
and in
Section

8.2
are usually different when
X
is a complete intersection.

We make the following conjecture.

Conjecture 8.2.5
.

If
X
is Fano, then the reduced and standard Gromov–Witten invariants agree.

We expect the invariants to be different if
X
is not Fano.

9.
Desingularizations in genus one

In genus one, reduced Gromov–Witten invariants were originally defined using the desingularization constructed in
[
VZ08
]
. It consists of a sequence of blow-ups determined by the geometry of the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
. In
[
HL10
]
, local equations for the blowup are determined. We aim to compare this desingularization with the one obtained using the Rossi blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathfrak{F}}\mathfrak{Pic}
, with
\mathfrak{F}
as in
Equation

26
. In particular, we describe
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathfrak{F}}\mathfrak{Pic}
in locally in the spirit of
[
HL10
]
.

9.0.1.
Charts

In genus one, the original definition of refined Gromov–Witten invariants comes from
[
VZ08
]
. The main is idea is to apply a sequence of blow ups to
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
in order to desingularize the main component. Strictly speaking, the sequence of blow ups takes place in the stack
\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}
of genus-1 prestable curves endowed with a weight. Let
\Theta_{k}
denote the closure of the loci in
\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}
of curves with
k
trees of rational curves attached to the core. Then one should blow up
\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}
along the loci
\Theta_{1}
,
\Theta_{2}
,
\Theta_{3}
and so on in order to produce a stack
\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{1}^{wt}
. This process induces a blowup
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
via fibre product.

Given a stratum
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)_{\gamma}
corresponding to a weighted graph
\gamma
, local equations of
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
and the local description of
\Theta_{k}
in that stratum are described explicitly in
[
HL10
]
. The purpose of this section is to summarize such local description, give coordinates for the new approach locally and compare both.

It may be helpful to keep in mind the following diagram, described below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F=0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Phi_{\gamma}=0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{D}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{1}^{wt}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}

Fix a weighted graph
\gamma
with root
o
. Let
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)
,
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}
and
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{*}
denote the vertices, the terminal vertices (or leaves) and the non-rooted vertices of
\gamma
, respectively. We take the natural ordering in
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)
making the root
o
the minimal element. We assume that the weight in
\gamma
is non-negative on every vertex and that
\gamma
is terminally weighted, meaning that the vertices with non-zero weights are exactly those in
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}
is the stack of genus-1 prestable curves endowed with a weight. Remember that every element
C
parametrized by
\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}
has a dual (weighted) graph
\gamma
, which can be made terminally weighted and rooted by first declaring the root to be the (contraction of) the core of
C
and then pruning along all non-terminal positively weighted nodes. We will denote by
o
the root of any terminally weighted rooted tree, and by
a,b,\ldots
the remaining vertices.

In the diagram,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{M}}_{1}^{wt}
denotes the blow up of
\mathfrak{M}_{1}^{wt}
described above and
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
is the stack of stable pairs
(C,D)
with
D
an effective Cartier divisors supported in the smooth locus of
C
. Fix a point
(C,D)
in
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
and a map in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
with underlying curve
C
. Then
U
is a small open around the fixed map in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
,
\mathcal{V}
is a smooth chart around the point
(C,D)
in
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
containing the image of
U
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the total space of the sheaf
\rho_{\ast}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})^{\oplus n}
on
\mathcal{V}
.

Let
V_{\gamma}=\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{*}}\mathbb{A}^{1}
be an affine space that serves as model for local equations. We denote by
z_{a},z_{b},\ldots
the natural coordinates in
V_{\gamma}
. Similarly,
E_{\gamma}=V_{\gamma}\times\prod_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{r}
and the coordinates on the affine space
(\mathbb{A}^{1})^{r}
corresponding to
a\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}
will be denoted by
w_{a,1},\ldots,w_{a,r}
. The ideal
\Phi_{\gamma}=(\Phi_{\gamma,1},\ldots,\Phi_{\gamma,r})
will be described explicitly in
Equation

73
. The smooth morphism
\phi
comes from the natural coordinates on
\mathcal{V}
, associated to the smoothing of each of the disconnecting nodes in
C
(which are in natural bijection with
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{*}
). The map
\mathcal{U}\to(F=0)
is an open embedding. Finally,
\widetilde{\phi}
is induced by
\phi
and
F=\widetilde{\phi}^{*}\Phi_{\gamma}
. It is in this sense that we can think of
\Phi_{\gamma}
as the equations of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)_{\gamma}
.

Following
[
HL10
]
, given a terminally weighted rooted graph
\gamma
, the ideal
\Phi_{\gamma}=(\Phi_{\gamma,1},\ldots,\Phi_{\gamma,r})
inside
V_{\gamma}
can be described as

(73)

\Phi_{\gamma,i}=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}z_{\left[v,o\right]}w_{v,i}\quad 1\leq i\leq r,

where

z_{\left[v,o\right]}=\prod_{o\prec a\preceq v}z_{a}.

Note that, for fixed
i
, the variables
w_{a,i}
only appear in the
i
-th equation
\Phi_{\gamma,i}
. Due to the symmetry of the equations and the fact that all blow ups take place in
V_{\gamma}
, which has coordinates
\{z_{a}\}_{a\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{*}}
(but not the
w_{a,i}
), in the examples below we will not write down the index
i
in the equations
\Phi_{\gamma,i}
nor in the variables
w_{a,i}
. For example, in the study of the equations
\Phi_{\gamma,i}
after blowing up, it will be clear that the index
i
is irrelevant, in the sense that the way that
\Phi_{\gamma,i}
changes is independent of
i
.

9.1.
Local equations of desingularizations

The local equations of the loci that must be blown up are described, following
[
HL10
]
.

Firstly, we describe how to assign an ideal
I_{\gamma}
to any semistable terminally weighted rooted tree
\gamma
. Here,
semistability
of
\gamma
means that every non-root vertex with weight zero has at least two edges.

The
trunk
of
\gamma
is the maximal chain
o=v_{0}\prec v_{1}\prec\ldots\prec v_{r}
of vertices in
\gamma
such that each vertex
v_{i}
with
1\leq i<r
has exactly one immediate descendant
and
v_{r}
is called a
branch vertex
if is it not terminal. Note that
\gamma
is a path tree if and only if it has no branch vertex.

Definition 9.1.1
.

Let
\gamma
be a semistable terminally weighted rooted tree with branch vertex
v
and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
be the immediate descendants of
v
. To
\gamma
we associate the ideal

I_{\gamma}=(z_{a_{1}},\ldots,z_{a_{k}})

in
V_{\gamma}
.

First, we must
blow up
V_{\gamma}
along the ideal
I_{\gamma}
. To describe the remaining steps we need to introduce the following operations.

Definition 9.1.2
.

Let
\gamma
be a terminally weighted semistable rooted tree.

•

The
pruning
of
\gamma
along a vertex
v
is the new tree obtained by removing all the descendants of
v
(and the corresponding edges) and declaring the weight of
v
to be the sum of the original weight of
v
plus the weights of all removed vertices.

•

The
advancing
of a vertex
v
with immediate ascendant
\overline{v}
in
\gamma
is a new tree obtained by replacing every edge
(\overline{v},v^{\prime})
with
v^{\prime}\neq v
by an edge
(\overline{v},v)
and pruning along all positively weighted non-terminal vertices as long as possible. In
Section

9.1.4
we will denote by
\gamma_{v}
the advancing of
v
in
\gamma
and by
\gamma_{v}^{\prime}
the same tree before pruning.

•

Suppose
\gamma
has a branch vertex
v
. A
monoidal transform
of
\gamma
is a tree obtained by advancing one of the immediate descendants of
v
. The set of monoidal transforms of
\gamma
is
\mathrm{Mon}(\gamma)
.

It turns out that the ideal
\Phi_{\gamma}
behaves nicely under monoidal transforms. Indeed, let
\gamma
be a semistable terminally weighted rooted tree with branch vertex
v
and let
a=a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}
be the immediate descendants of
v
. Let
\gamma_{a}
be the tree advancing of
a
in
\gamma
. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}\to V_{\gamma}
be the blow-up of
V_{\gamma}
along the ideal
I_{\gamma}=(z_{a_{1}},\ldots,z_{a_{k}})
. We view
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
embedded inside
V_{\gamma}\times\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
. There is a natural way to associate to each generator
z_{a_{i}}
of
I_{\gamma}
one chart of
\mathbb{P}^{k-1}
, and thus also of
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
. We denote such chart by
\widetilde{V}_{\gamma,a_{i}}
. Let
\pi_{a}\colon\widetilde{V}_{\gamma,a}\to V_{\gamma}
be the restriction of the natural projection, where
a=a_{1}
. Then, by the proof of
[
HL10
, Lemma 5.14]
, one of the following must hold

•

either
\gamma_{a}
is a path tree, and then the zero locus of
\pi_{a}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})
has smooth components;

•

or
\gamma_{a}
is not a path tree and then

(74)

\pi_{a}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=\Phi_{\gamma_{a}}.

The whole blow-up process is summarized as follows.
Fix
\gamma
. First blow up
V_{\gamma}
along
I_{\gamma}
. The pullback of
\Phi_{\gamma}
is controlled by
\mathrm{Mon}(\gamma)
. If
\mathrm{Mon}(\gamma)
consists only of path trees, we are done. Otherwise, for every element
\gamma^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Mon}(\gamma)
which is not a path tree, blow up the chart of
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
corresponding to
\gamma^{\prime}
along
I_{\gamma^{\prime}}
. Continue recursively. The process concludes by
[
HL10
, Lemma 3.12]
.

Now we want to describe
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathfrak{F}}\mathfrak{Pic}
locally. Namely, we want to describe which loci inside
\mathfrak{Pic}
we are blowing up locally. We have an exact sequence

0\to\rho_{*}\mathcal{L}\to\rho_{*}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\rho_{*}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\mid_{\mathcal{A}}

by
Equation

35
. The change of notation is due to the fact that
Equation

35
was global in
\mathfrak{Pic}
, but we now work locally.

After a careful study of the second morphism,
[
HL10
, Theorem 4.16]
concludes that
\rho_{\ast}\mathcal{L}
is the direct sum of a trivial bundle with the kernel of the morphism

(75)

\bigoplus_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}\varphi_{v}\colon\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\oplus\ell}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}},

where
\ell
is the cardinality of
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}
and
\varphi_{v}=\prod_{o\prec v^{\prime}\preceq v}\zeta_{v}
, with
\zeta_{v}
the smoothing parameter of the disconnecting node corresponding to the vertex
v
.

By
Proposition

7.1.3
, the sheaf
\mathfrak{F}
can be described locally as the dual of
Equation

75
. In particular, by
Remark

9.1.6
we have that
\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathfrak{F}}\mathfrak{Pic}
agrees, locally, with the blowup along the ideal generated by the entries
(\varphi_{v})_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}
. In local coordinates, this ideal can be described as follows.

Definition 9.1.3
.

Let
\gamma
be a semistable terminally weighted rooted tree with
\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{t}\}
. To
\gamma
we associate the ideal

J_{\gamma}=(z_{[v_{1},o]},\ldots,z_{[v_{t},o]}).

Similarly to
Equation

74
, in the same setup we have that

\pi_{a}^{*}(J_{\gamma})=J_{\gamma_{a}},

independently of whether
\gamma_{a}
is has a branch vertex. This follows again from the proof of
[
HL10
, Lemma 5.14]
.

9.1.4.
Examples

For two concrete trees
\gamma
, we compute the equations
\Phi_{\gamma}
as well as the ideals
I_{\gamma}
and
J_{\gamma}
. We describe the blow up process of Hu and Li and show that the result indeed desingularizes the main component of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
locally. Furthermore, we check that the ideal
J_{\gamma}
becomes locally principal in Hu–Li’s blow-up.

Example 9.1.5
.

Consider the following labelled graph:

\gamma
=

\Tree

[.
o

a
[.
b

c

d
]]

\displaystyle\Phi_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=z_{a}w_{a}+z_{b}(z_{c}w_{c}+z_{d}w_{d}),

\displaystyle I_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=(z_{a},z_{b}),

\displaystyle J_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=(z_{a},z_{b}z_{c},z_{b}z_{d}).

Let
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
be the blow up along
I_{\gamma}
, that is the zero locus of
z_{a}z_{b}^{\prime}-z_{b}z_{a}^{\prime}
inside
\mathbb{A}^{4}_{z_{a},z_{b},z_{c},z_{d}}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}_{z_{a}^{\prime},z_{b}^{\prime}}
. The chart associated to
a
is that where
z_{a}^{\prime}\neq 0
. Dehomogenizing amounts to the change of variables
z_{b}=z_{b}^{\prime}z_{a}
. By doing so, we get that

\pi_{a}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=z_{a}(w_{a}+z_{b}^{\prime}(z_{c}w_{c}+z_{d}w_{d}))

and that

\pi_{a}^{*}(J_{\gamma})=(z_{a},z_{a}z_{b}^{\prime}z_{c},z_{a}z_{b}^{\prime}z_{d})=(z_{a}).

This means that the zero locus of
\pi_{a}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})
already has smooth components, so no further blowups are needed on this chart, and that
\pi_{a}^{*}(J_{\gamma})
is principal on this chart too.

Below are the trees
\gamma_{a}^{\prime}
obtained by advancing
a
without pruning, and
\gamma_{a}
obtained by advancing
a
. We know that
\pi_{a}^{*}J_{\gamma}=J_{\gamma_{a}}
, but we check it in this example.

\gamma_{a}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
a
[.
b

c

d
]]]

\gamma_{a}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
a
]]

\displaystyle J_{\gamma_{a}}

\displaystyle=(z_{a}).

Similarly, we now look at the chart associated to
b
, where
z_{b}^{\prime}\neq 0
. The change of variables is now
z_{a}=z_{b}z_{a}^{\prime}
. It follows that

\pi_{b}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=z_{b}(z_{a}^{\prime}w_{a}+z_{c}w_{c}+z_{d}w_{d})

and that

\pi_{b}^{*}(J_{\gamma})=(z_{b}z_{a}^{\prime},z_{b}z_{c},z_{b}z_{d})=z_{b}(z^{\prime}_{a},z_{c},z_{d}).

This means that we still need to blow up. This time the tree
\gamma_{b}
obtained by advancing
b
in
\gamma
(no pruning is needed) is not a path tree. We also check the identities
J_{\gamma_{a}}=\pi_{a}^{*}J_{\gamma}
and
\pi_{b}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=\Phi_{\gamma_{b}}
.

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
a
] [.
c
] [.
d
]]]

\displaystyle\Phi_{\gamma_{b}}

\displaystyle=z_{b}(z_{a}w_{a}+z_{c}w_{c}+z_{d}w_{d}),

\displaystyle I_{\gamma_{b}}

\displaystyle=(z_{a},z_{c},z_{d}),

\displaystyle J_{\gamma_{b}}

\displaystyle=z_{b}(z_{a},z_{c},z_{d}).

To conclude the example, we need to blow up along the ideal
(z_{a},z_{c},z_{d})
. We collect the result below.

Advancing
a
, or equivalently looking at the chart
z_{a}^{\prime}\neq 0
, we have

\gamma_{b,a}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
a
[.
c
] [.
d
]]]]

\gamma_{b,a}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
a
]]]

\displaystyle\pi_{a}^{*}\pi_{b}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=\pi_{a}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma_{b}})

\displaystyle=z_{a}z_{b}(w_{a}+z_{c}w_{c}+z_{d}w_{d}),

\displaystyle J_{\gamma_{b,a}}

\displaystyle=(z_{a}z_{b}).

Advancing
c
, or equivalently looking at the chart
z_{c}^{\prime}\neq 0
, we have

\gamma_{b,c}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
c
[.
a
] [.
d
]]]]

\gamma_{b,c}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
c
]]]

\displaystyle\pi_{c}^{*}\pi_{b}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=\pi_{c}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma_{b}})

\displaystyle=z_{b}z_{c}(z_{a}w_{a}+w_{c}+z_{d}w_{d}),

\displaystyle J_{\gamma_{b,c}}

\displaystyle=(z_{b}z_{c}).

And finally, advancing
d
, or equivalently looking at the chart
z_{d}^{\prime}\neq 0
, we have

\gamma_{b,d}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
d
[.
a
] [.
c
]]]]

\gamma_{b,d}^{\prime}
=

\Tree

[.
o
[.
b
[.
d
]]]

\displaystyle\pi_{d}^{*}\pi_{b}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma})=\pi_{d}^{*}(\Phi_{\gamma_{b}})

\displaystyle=z_{b}z_{d}(z_{a}w_{a}+z_{c}w_{c}+w_{d}),

\displaystyle J_{\gamma_{b,d}}

\displaystyle=(z_{b}z_{d}).

Remark 9.1.6
.

Example

9.1.5
shows that the Rossi blow-up process of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)
is not equal to the Vakil–Zinger blow-up. This is compatible with Remark 4.4. in
[
HN19
]
. Indeed, the Rossi blowup around
\gamma
is given by
\mathrm{Bl}_{J_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}
and the Vakil–Zinger one is the iterated blow-up
\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma_{b}}}\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}
. We know there is a natural morphism

\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma_{b}}}\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}\to\mathrm{Bl}_{J_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}

over
V_{\gamma}
, either by
Proposition

4.4.6
or because we checked that
J_{\gamma}
pulls back to a principal ideal in
\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma_{b}}}\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}
. By contradiction, if there is a morphism

f\colon\mathrm{Bl}_{J_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}\to\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma_{b}}}\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}

over
V_{\gamma}
, then we get a morphism

\widetilde{f}\colon\mathrm{Bl}_{J_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}\to\mathrm{Bl}_{I_{\gamma}}V_{\gamma}

over
V_{\gamma}
. By
[
Moo01
]
, there is a fractional ideal
K
in
V_{\gamma}
and a positive integer
\alpha
such that

I_{\gamma}\cdot K=J_{\gamma}^{\alpha}.

This is not true for
I_{\gamma}=(z_{a},z_{b})
and
J_{\gamma}=(z_{a},z_{b}z_{c},z_{b}z_{d})
in
V_{\gamma}=\mathbb{A}^{4}_{z_{a},z_{b},z_{c},z_{d}}
.

.

\displaystyle J_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=(z_{a}z_{c},z_{a}z_{d},z_{b}z_{e},z_{b}z_{f}).

After blowing up, there are two charts, corresponding to the advacings of

and

respectively.

9.1.8.
Smoothness

In genus one,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{1,n}(\mathbb{P}^{r},d)\times_{\mathfrak{Pic}}\mathrm{Bl}_{\mathfrak{F}}\mathfrak{Pic}
has simple normal crossings following the same argument as in
[
HL10
, Theorem 5.24]
. It is enough to show that the zero locus of the ideal
\Phi_{\gamma}
becomes a simple normal crossing in the blow-up
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
of
V_{\gamma}
along the ideal
J_{\gamma}
.

Remember that
\Phi_{\gamma}=(\Phi_{\gamma,1},\ldots,\Phi_{\gamma,r})
with

\Phi_{\gamma,i}=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}z_{\left[v,o\right]}w_{b,i}\quad 1\leq i\leq r,

where

z_{\left[v,o\right]}=\prod_{o\prec a\preceq v}z_{a},

and that

J_{\gamma}=(z_{[v,o]})_{v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}.

For given
v^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}
, the pullback of the equation
\Phi_{\gamma,i}
on the chart corresponding to
v^{\prime}
is equal to

z_{[v^{\prime},o]}\left(w_{v^{\prime},i}+\sum_{v^{\prime}\neq v\in\mathrm{Ver}(\gamma)^{t}}z_{\left[v,o\right]}w_{b,i}\right)

by
[
HL10
, Lemma 5.14]
. This proves the claim.

9.1.9.
Maps between blowups

By
Proposition

4.4.6
there is a morphism from Vakil–Zinger’s blowup to Rossi’s blowup. In genus one, we can check it locally: it is equivalent to the fact that the pullback of the ideal
J_{\gamma}
to each chart
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
of the Hu–Li blowup of
V_{\gamma}
is principal. We have checked this in
Example

9.1.5
and
Example

9.1.7
, More generally, we can give a proof for every
\gamma
as follows.

By
Equation

74
if
z_{a}
is any of the generators of
I_{\gamma}
, then
\pi_{a}^{*}(J_{\gamma})=J_{\gamma_{a}}
where
\gamma_{a}
is the advancing of
a
in
\gamma
. In particular, it is enough to show that all the (natural) charts of
\widetilde{V_{\gamma}}
correspond to path trees, which is proven in
[
HL10
, Lemma 3.14]
.
